# Design: Cowos, branded website + direct booking for independent coworking spaces

Generated by /office-hours on 2026-11-02
Branch: main
Repo: mathsyagiri-scrimpify/Cowos
Status: APPROVED, revised 2026-11-04 (metered slab pricing; pilot programme removed), revision 2 the same day (Fixed and Usage plans; seat maps at launch)
Mode: Startup

> **Revision 2, 2026-11-04 (product owner).** Two changes. (1) Tenants choose between two pricing plans (Rule #12): a **Fixed plan**, one flat monthly fee with unlimited server usage and messages included up to a monthly fair-use allowance, and a **Usage plan**, the metered slab billing of Rules #3–#6 with no monthly fee. Owners can switch plans; a switch takes effect on the 1st of the next month. Both plans include every feature, and 0% commission is unchanged. (2) Owners build a seat map for each space with a grid builder and templates, and customers pick their seat on it like a movie ticket (Rule #13). Both ship at launch. The build plan for a small team using Claude Code is `docs/requirements/14-build-plan.md`, with a launch target of week 20 [estimated]. Where this document or the pack says "no subscription", "no plans", "usage only" or "launch at week 24", revision 2 replaces it. Revision 2 has not yet been through the CEO, design or engineering reviews; the review sections below predate it.

> **Revision 2026-11-04 (product owner).** The free pilot programme (first 20 owners free for a year) is removed. SSPL charges each tenant only for metered consumption: server usage (requests and storage) and notifications (OTP, SMS, WhatsApp, email), each priced in slabs that SSPL configures, like an electricity bill. Hosting is one app server plus a managed database. Nothing else is charged. Where the review sections further down mention pilots, the free year, the AWS-cost equal split or the Billing release, this revision replaces them; see "What the revision replaces" at the end of Pricing and Billing Rules. All other review decisions stand.

## Problem Statement

Independent coworking spaces in India (1–3 locations) take bookings over WhatsApp, phone and spreadsheets, and pay aggregators a large commission for customers they could serve directly. Many also lack a credible website. Cowos gives each space its own branded website and booking app. Customers pay the space directly through the space's own Razorpay account, with whatever fees or charges the space chooses. Cowos takes 0% commission on bookings. Each space picks how it pays SSPL: a Fixed plan (one flat monthly fee, unlimited use, messages within a fair-use allowance) or a Usage plan (pay only for server usage and messages, priced in slabs like an electricity bill). Owners draw their space as a seat map, and customers pick a seat like a movie ticket. The full product definition is the requirements pack in `docs/requirements/` (start with `00-start-here.md`).

## Demand Evidence

- 1–3 casual conversations with coworking owners, plus SSPL's own thesis drawn from Directoo's restaurant model.
- No paid commitments, sign-ups, or workflow data from any named space yet.
- Status: **unverified**. The product owner has decided to build regardless (see Premise 3).

## Status Quo

Not observed directly. The owner's view ("Actually I dont know but all the 3 options could be the case"):
- Bookings over WhatsApp, calls and Excel, with no online payment.
- Aggregator listings that take commission and own the customer.
- A basic website, with enquiries still handled by hand.
- Existing coworking software is less common.

Landscape (web search, generic terms):
- A 2023 founder interview reports myHQ takes 25–30% per booking.
- India-first operations tools already exist (Meshfloor, Engage Apps, CoWorkingOps, Omnify), so "cheap + GST" is not a unique position.

## Target User & Narrowest Wedge

- **Beachhead:** independent 1–3 location coworking spaces in one city, with a meaningful share of bookings from aggregators. Hyderabad was suggested. The city is not yet confirmed.
- **Wedge:** the full website + booking app (owner's choice). No smaller paid wedge is planned.
- **Expansion gate:** open a second city when 10 tenants in the beachhead city each take 10 or more paid online bookings a month for 3 consecutive months.

## Constraints

- Fixed stack: ASP.NET Core (.NET LTS) API, React + TypeScript, PostgreSQL, AWS ap-south-1.
- "0% commission" means Cowos takes no share of a tenant's booking revenue. Customer pricing, promo codes and fees are the tenant's decision.
- Tenants create their own promo codes in the Tenant Admin.
- SSPL revenue model: each tenant chooses a Fixed plan (flat monthly fee, unlimited server usage, messages within a fair-use allowance) or a Usage plan (metered server usage and messages at slab prices SSPL configures). Both plans include every feature. No commission (Premise 5, Pricing and Billing Rule #12).
- Hosting: one app server for all tenants (API, admin portals, booking app, background worker, site renderer), a managed PostgreSQL database, and tenant websites on S3 + CloudFront (owner decision 2026-11-04).
- Team and timeline as in the pack: about 7–8 people, MVP in about 5–6 months.

## Premises

| # | Premise | Status |
|---|---------|--------|
| 1 | Cowos covers both pains: recovering aggregator commission and replacing WhatsApp/Excel/invoice admin | Revised by owner ("Both should be covered") |

| 5 | Each tenant chooses how it pays SSPL: a Fixed plan (one flat monthly fee, unlimited server usage, messages within a monthly fair-use allowance) or a Usage plan (metered server usage and notifications, each in slabs whose prices SSPL configures, like electricity units). Every feature is on both plans. No commission. Customers pay tenants whatever the tenant decides | Owner's decision (2026-11-04); the Fixed plan was added in revision 2 the same day (Rule #12). Replaces the 2026-11-02 version ("share of the real AWS cost, messaging at cost") and keeps D1 decided. SSPL sets the prices, so they may include a margin; that reverses the earlier accepted risk that the model can't fund support or development (pack risk R1) |

## Pricing and Billing Rules

Approved by the product owner on 2026-11-04. They replace the Pilot Rules of 2026-11-02.

| # | Topic | Rule |
|---|-------|------|
| 1 | Launch gate | Tenants go live on production only after the production entry gate passes: cross-tenant isolation suite green, pen test critical and high findings fixed, Razorpay Partner live, DLT/WhatsApp/SES approvals in, verified backups and point-in-time recovery with a documented restore drill, and counsel review of the ToS, DPA, and the privacy and refund policy templates Cowos generates for tenants. No earlier than week 18, likely week 20 with the small-team build plan (pack 14) [estimated]; week 28 with the full-team plan (pack 12 §3). Launch milestone: 10 tenants live (the pack's launch checklist) |
| 2 | Features | No paid tiers. Every tenant gets the full feature set, plus Phase 2 features as they ship |
| 3 | Meters | **Server, request units:** 1 unit = 1,000 page views and API calls for the tenant: page views are HTML document requests in the CloudFront logs of the tenant's website and booking-app hosts (images, CSS, JS, fonts and other static files are not counted), and API calls are `/api/*` requests in the same logs, so each is counted once (CEO review 2, C2-A1; eng re-review 3, R22). Only 2xx and 304 responses count (R21). Tenant Admin, Super Admin and support traffic is not metered (R22). Requests blocked by WAF and SSPL's own health checks and monitoring are not counted. **Server, storage units:** 1 unit = 1 GB-month of the tenant's stored media and files, averaged from daily snapshots. **Notifications:** 1 unit = 1 message, metered separately for OTP SMS, other SMS, WhatsApp and email. A WhatsApp message followed by its SMS fallback counts once on each meter. A message the provider did not charge SSPL for (WhatsApp not delivered within 24 hours, SMS rejected by DLT before submission) is credited back to the wallet (R23) |
| 4 | Slabs | Every meter has its own slab table, applied telescopically like an electricity bill: units in slab 1 at the slab-1 price, the next units at the slab-2 price, and so on. SSPL configures the slab boundaries and prices in a Super Admin rate card. A slab price can be ₹0, which gives a free allowance. Each rate card version has an effective date (the 1st of a month); invoices and wallet debits always use the version in force when the usage happened. Slab counts reset at the start of each calendar month, India time (IST) |
| 5 | Server billing | Monthly in arrears, calendar month in IST. The invoice is issued on the 1st for the previous month (usage is known, so there is nothing to wait for). On the Usage plan it has one line per server meter showing units and the slab breakdown; on the Fixed plan it has one Fixed plan line (Rule #12); GST is added. Due in 10 days. Paid by Razorpay payment link, or optional UPI Autopay or e-mandate (₹5,000 per-tenant maximum, RBI pre-debit notice). For Usage plan tenants the billing page shows units used per meter, the slab breakdown and a month-to-date estimate, updated daily. An invoice goes out only once every day of the month has been counted (C2-A2); Usage plan owners can set a usage alert, with a default at 50% above last month (C2-A3) |
| 6 | Notification billing | Prepaid wallet. Each message is debited at the slab price for its channel, based on the tenant's month-to-date count, in one atomic step with the count (eng R4). At zero balance, SMS and WhatsApp notifications fall back to email. OTPs and emails continue down to a −₹500 floor. What stops once the floor is reached is open (Open Question 4). The wallet page shows the month-to-date count and current slab per channel |
| 7 | Trial | New tenants get the 14-day trial from the pack: publish on a platform subdomain only, no plan fee and server usage not billed. Notifications need wallet balance from day one. A tenant becomes Active when it adds a payment method and chooses a plan (Rule #12) |
| 8 | Lifecycle | Unpaid server invoices follow BRD §7.3 from launch: grace, Restricted (website live, new online bookings paused), Suspended, retention, deletion |
| 9 | Onboarding | Tenants sign up self-serve on cowos.in from launch, with the onboarding wizard (4A). SSPL helps a tenant only through tenant-approved support grants (FR-SA-030). The "Book a demo" form (CEO-A2) stays as a lead channel. BR-05 paid assisted onboarding stays an open question (pack OQ12) |
| 10 | SSPL cost view | SSPL still loads AWS Data Exports (CUR 2.0) from the production account, but only for its own view in Super Admin: actual AWS and messaging-provider cost next to billed revenue per month, so it can set slab prices. CUR is not used to bill tenants |
| 11 | Hosting | One app server runs every tenant's API, admin portals, booking app, worker and renderer. PostgreSQL is managed (backups, point-in-time recovery). Tenant websites stay on S3 + CloudFront. The app server is a single point of failure for admin and booking: it needs automatic instance recovery, an infrastructure-as-code rebuild path and health alarms. The availability target and deploy downtime are open (Open Question 6) |
| 12 | Plans (revision 2) | Two plans, chosen by the Owner when the tenant becomes Active (Rule #7). Both include every feature (Rule #2) and 0% commission. **Fixed plan:** one flat monthly fee, the same for every Fixed tenant, set on each rate card version (so it changes only with a new version from a future 1st). Server usage (requests and storage) is unlimited and never billed or shown on the bill; the platform still counts it in the background for SSPL's cost view (Rule #10). Messages are included up to a monthly fair-use allowance per channel (OTP SMS, other SMS, WhatsApp, email) set on the rate card; above the allowance, each message is paid from the prepaid wallet at the Usage plan slab price for that month's count, under the Rule #6 wallet rules. The fee is billed in arrears on the monthly invoice on the 1st, prorated by billable day in the month the tenant becomes Active and the month it cancels; days in Suspended, PendingDeletion or after cancellation are not charged. **Usage plan:** no monthly fee; server usage and every message are metered and priced in slabs (Rules #3–#6). **Switching:** the Owner can switch at any time; the new plan applies from the 1st of the next month, so every calendar month has one plan and no mid-month proration is needed. The billing page shows what last month would have cost on the other plan. The fee and the allowances are set by the product owner before launch (Open Question 9) |
| 13 | Seat maps (revision 2) | Owners draw each space as a grid seat map in the Tenant Admin, from a template or a blank grid, painting desks, cabins, meeting rooms, aisles, walls and the entrance; seats are labelled automatically (A1, A2...). Each desk is a bookable seat and each cabin or meeting-room block is a bookable unit. Customers pick their seat on the map in the booking app (free, booked, out of service, your pick), or keep the one-tap "Any available seat". The full design is in "Seat maps (revision 2)" below. Ships at launch (pack 14 build plan, weeks 8–9) |

**What revision 2 replaces:**
- "No subscription, plan fee or plans" everywhere: tenants now choose a Fixed or Usage plan (Rule #12). There are still no feature tiers and no commission.
- Pack FR-TA-068 (floor image + pins, Phase 2) and the "Interactive floor-plan seat picker" deferral in pack 00 §2: replaced by the grid seat map at launch (Rule #13). A floor image behind the grid is a later option, not planned.
- Launch at week 24 (Approach A, pack 12): launch target week 20 with the small-team build plan (pack 14) [estimated].

**What the revision replaces in the review sections below:**

- Eng R1 (invoice on the 10th), R2 (CUR equal split by tenant-days), R9 (TODO to weight the split), R12 ("last month's actual" per-space number), R15 (daily estimate from CUR): replaced by rules #3–#5 and #10. The pricing page shows the current rate card instead.
- CEO-S1 and CEO-S2 (Billing release at launch + 4 months for billing, sign-up and the wizard): reversed. Without free pilots every tenant pays from day one, so billing, metering, the rate card, self-serve sign-up and the wizard ship at launch. The P1-B phase code is retired.
- CEO-S3 (2 themes at launch, themes 3–4 six weeks later): stays; validation is with 3 early tenant owners.
- CEO-A4 pilot scorecard and R20: become a tenant scorecard in Super Admin (all live tenants) with the same IST payment-capture month rule.
- CEO-A5 cost-recovery goal: G3 becomes "150 paying tenants; billed server and notification revenue covers SSPL's production AWS and messaging-provider cost by launch + 18 months", read from rule #10.
- CEO review A2 (Super Admin creates pilots, Owner invite), R19 (pre-handover setup grant) and re-review 2 D1's "Create pilot" tab: removed with the pilot programme. The Super Admin "Tenants" area keeps Pilot requests (renamed Demo requests) and the scorecard.
- Hosting: the pack's ECS Fargate services become one app server (rule #11); eng R3, R5–R8, R10, R11, R13, R14, R16, R17, R18 and CEO-A1–A3 stand.

## Approaches Considered

### Approach A: Full MVP as specified (chosen)
Build all of Phase 1 in the order of `docs/requirements/14-build-plan.md` (revision 2), with both plans, metered billing and seat maps in Phase 1; launch target week 20 with a small team using Claude Code [estimated]. Effort XL, risk medium-high.

### Approach B: Booking-first, then website builder
Not chosen. Spaces without a website would wait for the second release, delaying the full branded-site pitch.

### Approach C: Done-for-you onboarding first
Not chosen on 2026-11-02 (it was proposed for the free pilot programme, which no longer exists).

## Recommended Approach

**Approach A.** It delivers the complete suite in the build order the requirements pack already specifies, which is what the owner asked for.

Consequences to plan for:
- Metering, the rate card, rating, server invoices, the slab-priced wallet, dunning, self-serve sign-up and the wizard are all launch scope (rules #3–#9). This adds back the weeks the CEO review had freed before the launch gate.
- Every tenant pays from launch (after its trial), so SSPL carries no pilot-year cost. SSPL's revenue depends on the slab prices it sets (rule #10 shows cost next to revenue).
- One app server keeps hosting cost low but makes it a single point of failure (rule #11).
- Metered request billing means a tenant pays for traffic it didn't ask for (bots, scrapers) unless it is filtered; WAF-blocked requests are excluded, the rest is Open Question 3.
- The Fixed plan (Rule #12) gives SSPL a predictable monthly income from each Fixed tenant and gives owners a bill they can plan for; the Usage plan keeps the entry cost low for small spaces. The Fixed plan reuses the rate card, the invoice and the wallet already designed, so it adds little build time. The risk is a very busy Fixed tenant costing SSPL more than its fee; the cost view (Rule #10) shows this per tenant, and the message allowance caps the biggest variable cost.
- The seat map (Rule #13) is the largest addition: about 4 weeks of frontend and backend work in the full-team plan. In the small-team build plan (pack 14) it is a 2-week slice for two developers in weeks 8–9.

## Seat maps (revision 2)

Owners told SSPL that describing their own space is the hardest part of setting up a booking app: every coworking space has a different shape and seating. The seat map makes that a 10-minute job and lets customers choose a seat the way they choose a movie seat.

**Owner builds the map (Tenant Admin → Inventory → Seat maps):**
1. Pick the location and add a map per floor or zone ("Ground floor · Main hall").
2. Start from a template (Rows of desks, Pods of 4, Cabins along a wall, Meeting room + desks, Blank) or set the grid size (rows × columns, up to 40 × 60).
3. Paint with tools: Desk (1 cell = 1 seat), Cabin and Meeting room (rectangular blocks), Aisle, Wall, Entrance, Text label. Tap one cell or drag across many; select an area to copy, move or clear; undo and redo.
4. Seats are labelled automatically by row letter and number (A1, A2...), skipping non-seat cells. The owner can rename any seat; labels are unique per location.
5. Assign: each desk belongs to a product (a hot desk pool or dedicated desks); each block is linked to a cabin or meeting-room unit. Painting creates the units automatically, the same as bulk create (pack FR-TA-062). Seats can carry tags (window, power, monitor, standing) and a status (active or out of service).
6. Preview as a customer, then "Publish map". A map has a draft and a live version; publishing makes the layout live in the booking app within a minute, without a site publish.

**Rules the builder enforces:** every seat is assigned to a product; no duplicate labels; blocks don't overlap. A seat or block with future bookings can't be deleted or reassigned: the editor lists those bookings so the owner can move them first. Bookings point at seat units, never at grid cells, so redrawing a map never breaks a booking. Every publish is audited. SSPL support can build a map for an owner only under an Owner-approved support grant (Rule #9).

**Hot desk pools with a map:** each hot desk product has a "Seat choice" setting: Off (a pool with no seats, as today), Optional (the default once a map exists) or Required. With a map, the pool's capacity is the number of active seats assigned to it. "Any available seat" assigns a seat at booking; the front desk can move it. When a publish turns seats on for a pool that already has future bookings, each of those bookings is given a free seat in the same transaction and the customer is told the seat; publish fails if any day has more bookings than seats (CEO review 3, R3-A1).

**Customer picks a seat (booking app):** after date, duration and time, "Choose your seat" shows the map: free seats green, booked grey, out of service hatched, the customer's pick gold, with a legend and the seat's tags on tap. Pinch to zoom and pan. "Any available seat" stays the one-tap default, so the fast path is not slower. For more than one seat the customer picks N seats; "Seat us together" ships after launch (R3-C4). A list view (seats grouped by row) offers the same choice to keyboard and screen-reader users. Picked seats are held with the existing 10-minute hold (pack FR-BA-017); a database exclusion constraint stops two bookings of one seat in overlapping times, and if someone took the seat during checkout the customer sees "A3 was just booked · pick another seat" with the map refreshed. The confirmation, desk pass and invoice show the seat label.

**Front desk:** at launch, staff move a booking to another free seat with "Change seat" on the booking detail (FR-TA-068f), and the customer is notified. The "Today on the map" view with drag-to-move ships after launch (CEO review 3, R3-C4).

**Limits:** up to 10 maps per location and 40 × 60 cells per map, the same for every tenant (Open Question 10).

**Not in this version:** a floor-plan photo behind the grid, free-form rotation of desks, and showing the map on the public website.

**Requested change, 2026-11-11 (pack v0.5, not reviewed):** the tenant sets the number of floors and uploads a plan per floor to trace over in the builder, defines seat types (open, partition, cubicle) with their own prices, and customers see a tab per floor with prices in the legend. This reverses "a floor-plan photo behind the grid" above. The requirements are in the pack (00 §C15; 04 FR-TA-068g to 068l, FR-BA-013a). This design doc has not been revised or re-reviewed for it.

## Open Questions

1. Which city is the beachhead? Hyderabad was suggested, not confirmed.
2. Slab boundaries and prices for each meter: the product owner will configure them in the rate card before launch.
3. Should requests from known bots and crawlers that pass WAF be billed as request units?
4. When a wallet reaches the −₹500 floor, which messages stop (OTP email, booking confirmations, invoices)?
5. How much notice does a tenant get before a new rate card version takes effect?
6. What availability target does the single app server need to meet, and is a few minutes of downtime per deploy acceptable?
7. Metering details raised while updating the pack (pack OQ26-OQ34): rounding of partial units and price precision; whether Trial usage counts toward slabs; late CloudFront logs vs rating on the 1st; ₹0 invoices and SSPL test tenants; what counts as stored files; invoices above the ₹5,000 e-mandate maximum; GST on wallet top-ups; retention of raw CloudFront logs (visitor IPs); whether Super Admin and support requests count.
8. D1 stays decided (revised by Premise 5, and by revision 2 for the two plans). D2 (hosting) was decided on 2026-11-04 (Rule #11). D3–D16 in `docs/requirements/13-risks-open-questions-decisions.md` are still Proposed, and D3–D6 block engineering.
9. The Fixed plan fee and its monthly message allowances per channel (Rule #12): the product owner sets them in the rate card before launch.
10. Seat map limits (Rule #13): are 10 maps per location and 40 × 60 cells per map enough for the largest beachhead spaces?

## Success Criteria

Proposed for the owner to confirm:
- 10 tenants live on production at launch (the pack's launch checklist).
- At least 60% of active tenants take 10 or more paid online bookings per month by month 6 after launch (goal G2 in `01-product-vision-and-scope.md`).
- Billed plan fees, server and notification revenue covers SSPL's production AWS and messaging-provider cost by launch + 18 months (goal G3).

## Distribution Plan

Cowos is a hosted web service. Tenants sign up on the Cowos marketing site from launch; the "Book a demo" form brings in leads SSPL follows up. Build and deploy follow the CI/CD design in `docs/requirements/08-architecture.md` §15, as updated for the single app server.

## Dependencies

- Decisions D2–D6 (hosting model, payments, domains, badge, database tenancy). D1 (pricing) is decided by Premise 5.
- Long-lead approvals: Razorpay Partner application, TRAI DLT registration, WhatsApp templates, SES production access, domain purchase.
- First tenant recruitment in the beachhead city, which runs in parallel with the build.
- Slab prices configured by the product owner before launch (Open Question 2).

## The Assignment

Talk to the first 5 coworking spaces in the beachhead city that could become launch tenants. For each one, record its booking channels, monthly booking volume, the aggregator fees it pays today and its expected monthly traffic and message volume (to test the slab prices), using the interview fields in `docs/HANDOFF.md` §5. Save the results to `docs/research/owner-interviews.md`. These five conversations are the first real demand evidence the product will have. (Revised 2026-11-04: the free pilot programme is gone.)

## What I noticed about how you think

- On demand, you said "We want to build the complete product suite irrespective of the demand." You hold a clear conviction about the full Directoo-style bundle and are willing to own that risk openly.
- When asked to pick one pain, you answered "Both should be covered" rather than accepting a narrower framing.
- On the beachhead, you said "Agree but we want to pilot with first 20 coworking owners for one year free." You accepted the focus and added your own go-to-market move on top.
- On the status quo, you said "Actually I dont know but all the 3 options could be the case." That honesty about what isn't known yet is the right starting point for the pilot interviews.

<!-- gstack:office-hours:concerns:start -->
## Reviewer Concerns

Disposition: CONCERNS_RECORDED

Stop: CONVERGENCE

### R3-1 — feasibility (blocking)

**Problem**

> Premise 5 replaces fixed plans with a variable monthly charge: that month's AWS bill, taken from the Cost and Usage Report, divided by active tenants. The design does not say how this charge is collected or when. The pack's platform billing is built on Razorpay Subscriptions with UPI Autopay, e-mandate or card (00 §C4, 12 §2). Subscriptions charge a fixed plan amount each cycle, so a variable amount needs per-cycle add-ons or a different recurring-charge mechanism, with a mandate maximum and RBI pre-debit notice. The month's AWS bill is final only after the month ends, so the charge is in arrears. Yet the design gives no invoice date, due date, or estimate-and-true-up rule, and it says 'no subscription' while still relying on dunning (Pilot Rules #7). There is also a clash for non-pilot tenants who start paying after the 14-day trial: BRD §7.3 enables Activation, and with it custom domains, only when the first invoice is paid. With billing in arrears, that first invoice arrives about a month after the trial ends.

**Remedy**

> Choose the collection mechanism: a Razorpay Subscription with a nominal plan plus per-cycle add-ons, token or e-mandate recurring charges, or invoice plus payment link. State the billing timing (arrears, or an estimate with a true-up), the invoice and due dates, and the mandate maximum. Redefine what moves a non-pilot from Trial to Active (for example, setting up a mandate or payment method) so that custom domains and indexing are not delayed until the first arrears invoice is paid.

### R3-2 — completeness (blocking)

**Problem**

> Pilot Rules #10 defines the charge as the platform's AWS bill divided by active tenants, but leaves both inputs undefined. The cost pool: does it include only production, or also the staging account (+30–40% per 08 §14), dev, CI, the marketing site and Super Admin? Are credits, Savings Plans and taxes in it? The denominator: 'active' is not mapped to the FR-PL-062 states. It is unclear whether Trial, PastDue, Restricted, Suspended or retention-period tenants count, how SSPL's own test tenant and demo tenants count, and whether a tenant live for part of a month is prorated. Each choice changes every tenant's invoice. For example, excluding trial and unpaid tenants makes paying tenants fund their AWS costs, which conflicts with charging only 'their share'.

**Remedy**

> Define the cost pool (which AWS accounts and cost lines are included) and the denominator (which tenant states count, how partial months are handled, and how SSPL's test and demo tenants are treated). Also state who absorbs the share of tenants that don't pay.

### R3-3 — consistency (blocking)

**Problem**

> S3 storage, CloudFront transfer and SES email are already in the AWS bill that is split equally. Charging a tenant again for 'storage over quota' bills that cost twice, unless the over-quota part is first removed from the shared pool. That contradicts 'only their share of the real AWS hosting cost'. The quotas and 'fair use' thresholds for non-pilot tenants are also undefined. FR-PL-030 has a per-tenant storage quota but no value, and the only fair-use cap in the design is the pilot messaging cap.

**Remedy**

> State the storage quota and any other fair-use thresholds that apply to all tenants. Specify that usage billed per tenant is removed from the pool before the equal split, or drop the separate usage line, so that no AWS cost is charged twice.

### R3-4 — completeness (blocking)

**Problem**

> The messaging billing model is unclear. The pack uses a prepaid wallet debited per message (BRD §7.1, FR-PL-040, FR-TA-175), and pilots use an SSPL-funded wallet (Pilot Rules #4). Pilot Rules #10, however, puts 'messaging at cost' on the monthly charge, after usage 'beyond fair use'. The design does not say whether non-pilot messaging is prepaid or added to the monthly invoice afterwards. It doesn't say what happens at zero balance, or whether non-pilots get any free messaging allowance. It also doesn't say who pays for customer sign-in OTPs for non-pilot tenants: 11-integrations §3 and D7 had them 'absorbed by the platform plan', and there is no plan any more.

**Remedy**

> Decide between a prepaid wallet and charging messaging on the monthly invoice, and state the behavior at zero balance or unpaid usage (for example, falling back to email). State whether non-pilots get a messaging allowance, and say who pays for sign-in OTPs, and at what rate, now that no plan absorbs them.

### R3-5 — feasibility (blocking)

**Problem**

> The production entry gate now likely passes at week 24, but Pilot Rules #1 and the Success Criteria still require 10 pilots live on production at the week-24 launch. If pilots can't be set up on production until the gate passes, all 10 must go live in the same week. Each is set up fresh, at about half a day plus 2 hours, which is roughly 7.5 person-days for one onboarding owner. Each also needs SSPL's first-publish review (4-working-hour target) and owner-side Razorpay OAuth (which needs Partner approval) and DNS changes. The design doesn't say whether a production tenant can be created and configured, unpublished and with no live customer bookings, before the gate passes, or which gate items block only go-live.

**Remedy**

> State which steps can happen on production before the gate passes (for example, tenant creation and setup through a support grant, unpublished, with no live bookings) and which steps the gate blocks. If that still doesn't fit, move the '10 pilots live' launch criterion, or the launch date, to a realistic point after the gate.

### R3-6 — completeness (blocking)

**Problem**

> Pilots need no payment method or mandate during the free period (Pilot Rules #4), but #6 expects them to pay a monthly hosting share from month 13. The design doesn't say when or how the payment method is collected (for example, required with the 60-day notice), or when the first charge is invoiced, given that billing is in arrears. Now that there is no plan to pick, 'unconverted pilot' is undefined. It could be a pilot that never pays its first charge, or any former pilot that misses a payment. That decides whether a pilot that pays month 13 and then misses a later month gets the 90-day Restricted period or the standard path.

**Remedy**

> Define 'unconverted': for example, a pilot that has not paid its first post-free-year charge. State when and how the payment method or mandate is collected before month 13, when the first charge is invoiced and due, and which lifecycle applies to a former pilot that misses a later payment.

### R3-7 — completeness (blocking)

**Problem**

> Persisting from R2-2. The design now says sign-in OTPs are exempt from the notification cap and have their own thresholds, but it still doesn't say whether a WhatsApp message followed by an SMS fallback (FR-PL-040) counts as one message or two. The same question applies to the OTP thresholds when the WhatsApp OTP channel falls back. The ₹1.8 lakh ceiling holds only if every message sent is counted.

**Remedy**

> State that each message sent, including a fallback SMS, counts toward the notification cap and the OTP thresholds, or adjust the cost ceiling to allow for fallbacks.

### R3-8 — completeness (blocking)

**Problem**

> Persisting from R2-3. Pilot Rules #4 puts the tenant in Active only 'during' the free hosting period. Under #3, that period starts at the first live publish on production. The tenant's state between being created on production and its first publish is still undefined. If it is Trial, the 14-day trial expiry, the subdomain-only publish rule, and noindex (FR-TW-008) apply, which could block or delay the first publish that starts the free year.

**Remedy**

> State the pilot tenant's state from creation on production to first publish (for example, Active with a pilot flag, with the free-period end date set at first publish), and confirm that trial expiry and the trial publish restrictions don't apply.

### R3-9 — feasibility (blocking)

**Problem**

> Persisting from R2-1. The production entry gate now includes the isolation suite, the pen-test critical and high fixes, Razorpay Partner, the messaging approvals, and counsel review of the ToS and DPA. It still leaves out two items that the R2-1 remedy listed as the minimum and that 12 §4 requires before real customer data: verified backups and restore (12 §4: 'Backups, PITR and a full restore drill documented'), and counsel review of the privacy policy and refund policy that Cowos generates for tenants. The gate can pass as early as week 22, before the M6 DR drill.

**Remedy**

> Add verified backups/PITR with a restore drill, and counsel review of the privacy policy and refund policy templates, to the production entry gate. Alternatively, record their omission as an accepted risk signed off by the owner.

### R3-10 — feasibility (minor)

**Problem**

> Several running costs fall outside the AWS bill: Razorpay fees on SSPL's own platform-billing collections, CPaaS/DLT and WhatsApp BSP fixed fees, Google Places/Maps and GST lookup API usage, the pen test, third-party SaaS (monitoring, help desk) and domains. The accepted risk in Premise 5 names only support and development, so it is unclear whether these costs are also unrecovered by design.

**Remedy**

> List the non-AWS running costs. State whether they are excluded from the charge and carried by SSPL as part of the accepted risk, or passed through as additional disclosed lines.

### R3-11 — clarity (minor)

**Problem**

> Showing the AWS bill breakdown reveals SSPL's total platform cost and active tenant count to every tenant. If the breakdown is too granular, it could expose other tenants' usage. The design doesn't define what is shown.

**Remedy**

> Define the breakdown's granularity (for example, monthly totals by service category plus the tenant count) and state that it never shows other tenants' individual usage.

### R3-12 — feasibility (minor)

**Problem**

> The indicative figure is the fixed base cost (₹25,000–45,000) divided by 20. It leaves out staging, if staging is in the pool, and variable costs. In the early months, with about 10–12 live tenants, a non-pilot would pay roughly ₹2,000–4,500 a month, more than the pack's Growth plan (₹2,499). The amount would also change every month as the tenant count and the AWS bill move, and the design doesn't mention this.

**Remedy**

> Give the indicative charge at launch-time tenant counts, say whether the pool includes staging, and state how month-to-month changes are handled (for example, a cap, smoothing, or advance notice).

### R3-13 — consistency (minor)

**Problem**

> Open Question 3 asks only about the 1,000-message notification cap. The new OTP alert and pause thresholds (1,000 and 2,000 per pilot per month) are equally provisional, and they drive most of the ₹1.8 lakh messaging ceiling.

**Remedy**

> Extend Open Question 3 to cover the OTP thresholds, or mark them as provisional in Pilot Rules #5.

### R3-14 — consistency (minor)

**Problem**

> BRD §7.3 has a grace period of days 0–7 past due and Restricted on days 8–21. Pilot Rules #6 gives a 14-day grace. It is unclear whether the 14 days apply only to former pilots or to every tenant, and how they map onto the BRD's day counts.

**Remedy**

> State that the 14-day grace is a pilot-specific exception (or a change for all tenants), and give the resulting day-by-day timeline.

### R3-15 — completeness (minor)

Examples are the Pro API and HTML embed, static apex IPs, priority support and the Dedicated cell.

### Prior finding evidence

### Unresolved at the cap

1. R3-1 — feasibility (blocking): Premise 5 replaces fixed plans with a variable monthly charge: that month's AWS bill, taken from the Cost and Usage Report, divided by active tenants. The design does not say how this charge is collected or when. The pack's platform billing is built on Razorpay Subscriptions with UPI Autopay, e-mandate or card (00 §C4, 12 §2). Subscriptions charge a fixed plan amount each cycle, so a variable amount needs per-cycle add-ons or a different recurring-charge mechanism, with a mandate maximum and RBI pre-debit notice. The month's AWS bill is final only after the month ends, so the charge is in arrears. Yet the design gives no invoice date, due date, or estimate-and-true-up rule, and it says 'no subscription' while still relying on dunning (Pilot Rules #7). There is also a clash for non-pilot tenants who start paying after the 14-day trial: BRD §7.3 enables Activation, and with it custom domains, only when the first invoice is paid. With billing in arrears, that first invoice arrives about a month after the trial ends.
2. R3-2 — completeness (blocking): Pilot Rules #10 defines the charge as the platform's AWS bill divided by active tenants, but leaves both inputs undefined. The cost pool: does it include only production, or also the staging account (+30–40% per 08 §14), dev, CI, the marketing site and Super Admin? Are credits, Savings Plans and taxes in it? The denominator: 'active' is not mapped to the FR-PL-062 states. It is unclear whether Trial, PastDue, Restricted, Suspended or retention-period tenants count, how SSPL's own test tenant and demo tenants count, and whether a tenant live for part of a month is prorated. Each choice changes every tenant's invoice. For example, excluding trial and unpaid tenants makes paying tenants fund their AWS costs, which conflicts with charging only 'their share'.
3. R3-3 — consistency (blocking): S3 storage, CloudFront transfer and SES email are already in the AWS bill that is split equally. Charging a tenant again for 'storage over quota' bills that cost twice, unless the over-quota part is first removed from the shared pool. That contradicts 'only their share of the real AWS hosting cost'. The quotas and 'fair use' thresholds for non-pilot tenants are also undefined. FR-PL-030 has a per-tenant storage quota but no value, and the only fair-use cap in the design is the pilot messaging cap.
4. R3-4 — completeness (blocking): The messaging billing model is unclear. The pack uses a prepaid wallet debited per message (BRD §7.1, FR-PL-040, FR-TA-175), and pilots use an SSPL-funded wallet (Pilot Rules #4). Pilot Rules #10, however, puts 'messaging at cost' on the monthly charge, after usage 'beyond fair use'. The design does not say whether non-pilot messaging is prepaid or added to the monthly invoice afterwards. It doesn't say what happens at zero balance, or whether non-pilots get any free messaging allowance. It also doesn't say who pays for customer sign-in OTPs for non-pilot tenants: 11-integrations §3 and D7 had them 'absorbed by the platform plan', and there is no plan any more.
5. R3-5 — feasibility (blocking): The production entry gate now likely passes at week 24, but Pilot Rules #1 and the Success Criteria still require 10 pilots live on production at the week-24 launch. If pilots can't be set up on production until the gate passes, all 10 must go live in the same week. Each is set up fresh, at about half a day plus 2 hours, which is roughly 7.5 person-days for one onboarding owner. Each also needs SSPL's first-publish review (4-working-hour target) and owner-side Razorpay OAuth (which needs Partner approval) and DNS changes. The design doesn't say whether a production tenant can be created and configured, unpublished and with no live customer bookings, before the gate passes, or which gate items block only go-live.
6. R3-6 — completeness (blocking): Pilots need no payment method or mandate during the free period (Pilot Rules #4), but #6 expects them to pay a monthly hosting share from month 13. The design doesn't say when or how the payment method is collected (for example, required with the 60-day notice), or when the first charge is invoiced, given that billing is in arrears. Now that there is no plan to pick, 'unconverted pilot' is undefined. It could be a pilot that never pays its first charge, or any former pilot that misses a payment. That decides whether a pilot that pays month 13 and then misses a later month gets the 90-day Restricted period or the standard path.
7. R3-7 — completeness (blocking): Persisting from R2-2. The design now says sign-in OTPs are exempt from the notification cap and have their own thresholds, but it still doesn't say whether a WhatsApp message followed by an SMS fallback (FR-PL-040) counts as one message or two. The same question applies to the OTP thresholds when the WhatsApp OTP channel falls back. The ₹1.8 lakh ceiling holds only if every message sent is counted.
8. R3-8 — completeness (blocking): Persisting from R2-3. Pilot Rules #4 puts the tenant in Active only 'during' the free hosting period. Under #3, that period starts at the first live publish on production. The tenant's state between being created on production and its first publish is still undefined. If it is Trial, the 14-day trial expiry, the subdomain-only publish rule, and noindex (FR-TW-008) apply, which could block or delay the first publish that starts the free year.
9. R3-9 — feasibility (blocking): Persisting from R2-1. The production entry gate now includes the isolation suite, the pen-test critical and high fixes, Razorpay Partner, the messaging approvals, and counsel review of the ToS and DPA. It still leaves out two items that the R2-1 remedy listed as the minimum and that 12 §4 requires before real customer data: verified backups and restore (12 §4: 'Backups, PITR and a full restore drill documented'), and counsel review of the privacy policy and refund policy that Cowos generates for tenants. The gate can pass as early as week 22, before the M6 DR drill.

issues verified: 9 of 9

Verification is deterministic: a fix counts when its `applied_text` is in the current design and absent from the round-3 snapshot. It confirms applied edits, never a review verdict; no reviewer was launched past the cap.

# Engineering Review (plan-eng-review, 2026-11-03)

Target: `docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md` (approved design) with the requirements pack in `docs/requirements/`. Premise 5 and the Pilot Rules supersede the pack's flat-plan text.

## Scope record

feature answers: none proposed (binding: Premise 3 "complete product suite", Approach A "full MVP as specified"); structure: A Original arrangement (D1, answered 2026-11-03); accepted scope: full Phase 1 suite as specified in `12-release-plan.md`, 8 deployables (marketing, admin, ops and booking React apps; API; separate Hangfire worker; Node renderer; static tenant sites); pending remedies: R1, R2, R3, R4, R5, R6, R7

Scope Challenge result: scope accepted as-is.

Reality: PLAN_REALITY phase=eng rows=7/7 missing=none verdict=complete; unverified premises=3 (myHQ rate, competitor list, per-message rate); FALSE premise: invoice on the 5th (AWS bill finalizes 5–7 days into the month).

## Decision ledger

### F2 (no question): pack still states flat plans
Finding: 2, P2, confidence 8/10, docs/requirements/12-release-plan.md:30, reviewer: plan-eng-review
Plan baseline: design approved 2026-11-02 ("These change the pack, which must be updated to match", docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md:59)
Runtime evidence: pack text unchanged (12-release-plan.md:30 "Plans, trial, Razorpay Subscriptions"; 00-start-here.md:67 plan table)
State: approved
Actual answer: covered by design approval (Approve, 2026-11-02)
Accepted scope: update every pack section listed in the design's Pilot Rules header and Reviewer Concern R3-16 to hosting cost only
History: none

### R1: when the monthly hosting invoice is issued
Finding: 1, P1, confidence 9/10, docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md:72, reviewer: plan-eng-review
Plan baseline: Pilot Rules #10 "The invoice is issued on the 5th for the previous month, once the AWS bill is final, and is due in 10 days" (approved 2026-11-02)
Runtime evidence: AWS finalizes usage charges 5–7 days into the following month; support fees post on the 6th–7th; refunds and credits can still change a finalized report (AWS CUR docs; bill finalization timing article). No code exists.
Comparison grid:
| Choice | Current | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 invoice date | 5th | 10th, after bill/InvoiceID is populated | 5th | the day the bill finalizes (varies) |
| R1 data source | "final" bill | finalized bill | estimated charges | finalized bill |
| R1 late credits/fees | not handled | adjustment line on next invoice | adjustment line on next invoice | adjustment line on next invoice |
| R1 due date | 10 days after invoice | 10 days after invoice | 10 days after invoice | 10 days after invoice |
| R2–R7 | pending | pending | pending | pending |
Question D2:
D2 — R1: When should the monthly hosting invoice be issued?
Project/branch/task: main, engineering review of docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md (Pilot Rules #10).
ELI10: Tenants pay their share of last month's AWS bill. The design invoices on the 5th, but AWS only finishes the bill 5–7 days into the month, and support fees and credits arrive after that. Invoicing on the 5th would charge every tenant a number that can still change.
Stakes if we pick wrong: tenants get invoices that don't match the AWS bill shown on their billing page, which undercuts the "real cost, shown transparently" promise.
Recommendation: A because it bills a finished number on a predictable date and handles late AWS changes openly.
Note: options differ in kind, not coverage — no completeness score.
Pros / cons:
A) Invoice on the 10th (recommended)
  ✅ Uses the finalized bill, so the invoice matches the AWS breakdown tenants see
  ✅ Fixed, predictable date; late credits or fees appear as a clear adjustment line next month
  ❌ Tenants pay about 5 days later each month than in the current design
B) Keep the 5th, estimate
  ✅ Keeps the current date and cash timing for SSPL
  ✅ Simple monthly schedule with a true-up next month
  ❌ Every invoice is an estimate, and most months carry a correction line
C) Invoice when final
  ✅ Always uses the finished bill on the earliest possible day
  ✅ Fewer adjustment lines than B
  ❌ Invoice date moves every month, which is harder for tenants and dunning
Net: a 5-day later invoice against billing tenants a number that is still moving.
Header: Invoice date
Options:
A) Invoice on the 10th (recommended)
Issue on the 10th once AWS marks the bill final (bill/InvoiceID populated); later credits, refunds or support fees go on the next invoice as an adjustment line. Due 10 days after. Effort S (human: ~2h / CC: ~10min spec change). Low risk.
B) Keep the 5th, estimate
Issue on the 5th from estimated charges; the difference to the final bill goes on next month's invoice. Due 10 days after. Effort S (human: ~2h / CC: ~10min). Medium risk: estimates every month.
C) Invoice when final
Issue on whatever day AWS finalizes the bill (usually the 5th–7th); later credits as an adjustment line. Due 10 days after. Effort S (human: ~3h / CC: ~15min). Medium risk: variable dates.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Invoice on the 10th (recommended), D2 answered 2026-11-03
Accepted scope: Pilot Rules #10 invoices on the 10th once AWS marks the bill final (bill/InvoiceID populated), due 10 days after; later credits, refunds or support fees appear as an adjustment line on the next invoice; Pilot Rules #6 first post-pilot invoice moves to the 10th of month 14
History: baseline "issued on the 5th" (Pilot Rules #10, approved 2026-11-02)

### R2: how the monthly AWS cost becomes each tenant's hosting invoice
Finding: A1, P1, confidence 8/10, docs/requirements/08-architecture.md:103 and docs/requirements/09-data-model.md:273/275, reviewer: plan-eng-review
Plan baseline: Pilot Rules #10 charge rules (approved 2026-11-02) and R1 (D2, invoice on the 10th); pack modules and data model still describe plans and subscriptions
Runtime evidence: no code exists; 08:103 "Cowos plans, subscriptions, platform invoices, dunning, messaging wallet"; 09:273 PlatformPlan with monthly_price/annual_price; 09:275 PlatformSubscription with plan_version_id
Comparison grid:
| Choice | Current | A | B |
|---|---|---|---|
| R2 bill source | unspecified | AWS Data Exports (CUR 2.0) from the production account to S3, read by a worker job on the 10th | SSPL Finance enters the finalized AWS invoice total and service breakdown in Super Admin, AWS invoice PDF attached, second person approves |
| R2 late-change detection | none | job re-reads the prior month and writes an adjustment line if it changed | Finance must notice and enter an adjustment |
| R2 missing-data handling | none | invoices held, Finance alerted, daily retry | invoices wait until Finance enters the month |
| R2 data model (common to A and B) | PlatformPlan, PlatformSubscription | replace with BillingProfile (payment method/mandate), CostPool, TenantCostShare, HostingInvoice, AdjustmentLine | same as A |
| R1 invoice date | 10th (D2) | 10th (D2) | 10th (D2) |
| R3–R7 | pending | pending | pending |
Question D3:
D3 — R2: How should each month's AWS bill become tenant hosting invoices?
Project/branch/task: main, engineering review of docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md (Pilot Rules #10, 08-architecture.md §4, 09-data-model.md §3.11).
ELI10: Tenants now pay a share of the real AWS bill, but the design never says how the system learns that bill. It can read AWS's own cost export automatically each month, or SSPL Finance can type the total into the admin portal with the AWS invoice attached. Either way the old "plans and subscriptions" tables get replaced with cost-pool and invoice tables.
Stakes if we pick wrong: a wrong total means every tenant's invoice is wrong at once, the most visible failure of the "real cost, transparent" promise.
Recommendation: A because it reads AWS's own numbers, catches late corrections automatically, and needs no monthly manual step.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10
Pros / cons:
A) Automated AWS cost export (recommended)
  ✅ Uses AWS's own cost data, so the billing page breakdown matches AWS exactly
  ✅ Spots late credits or fees by re-reading last month and adds an adjustment line itself
  ❌ More to build and test: export setup, a parser job and failure alerts
B) Finance enters the total
  ✅ Much less to build; the AWS invoice PDF is attached for every tenant to see
  ✅ Two-person approval makes the monthly number auditable
  ❌ A monthly manual step; typos or a missed late credit hit every tenant's invoice
Net: about a week more engineering against a monthly manual step that can misbill everyone.
Header: AWS bill source
Options:
A) Automated AWS cost export (recommended)
AWS Data Exports (CUR 2.0) from the production account land in S3; on the 10th a worker job checks the bill is final, builds the cost pool, splits it by tenant-days, writes invoices, and re-reads the prior month for late changes. Missing data holds invoices and alerts Finance. Replaces plan tables with BillingProfile, CostPool, TenantCostShare, HostingInvoice, AdjustmentLine. Effort M (human: ~1 week / CC: ~5h). Low-medium risk.
B) Finance enters the total
SSPL Finance enters the finalized AWS total and service breakdown in Super Admin with the AWS invoice PDF; a second person approves; the system splits and invoices. Late credits need a manual adjustment entry. Same new tables as A. Effort S (human: ~2 days / CC: ~2h). Medium risk: monthly manual input.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Automated AWS cost export (recommended), D3 answered 2026-11-03
Accepted scope: AWS Data Exports (CUR 2.0) from the production account to S3; worker job MonthlyHostingBilling on the 10th checks bill/InvoiceID, builds CostPool (net of credits, service breakdown), splits by tenant-days into TenantCostShare (SSPL test/demo shares to SSPL, pilot shares absorbed), writes HostingInvoice (+GST), re-reads the prior month and writes an AdjustmentLine when it changed, holds invoices and alerts Finance with daily retry when data is missing; replace PlatformPlan/PlatformPlanVersion/PlatformSubscription with BillingProfile, CostPool, TenantCostShare, HostingInvoice, AdjustmentLine in 08-architecture.md §4 and 09-data-model.md §3.11

### R3: tenant setting and transactions on pooled database connections
Finding: A2, P2, confidence 8/10, docs/requirements/08-architecture.md:134 and :139, reviewer: plan-eng-review
Plan baseline: 08-architecture.md §5.2 RLS with `set_config('app.tenant_id', @tenantId, true)` per request/transaction; NFR-MT-02 (07-non-functional-requirements.md) two-layer isolation; RDS Proxy for pooling (08:313)
Runtime evidence: no code exists; policy text 08:134 "USING (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::uuid)" errors when the setting is absent; transaction-local settings end with each autocommit statement; session-level SET through RDS Proxy pins connections and can leak to the next borrower (AWS RDS Proxy best practices)
Comparison grid:
| Choice | Current | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R3 transaction scope | "per request/transaction" (unspecified) | every DB unit of work runs in an explicit transaction opened by middleware/interceptor; set_config(..., true) is its first statement | setting applied at connection open (session level), reset when the connection returns to the pool | unchanged wording |
| R3 policy when setting missing | error | current_setting('app.tenant_id', true) returns NULL, so zero rows (fail closed, no error) | error | error |
| R3 guard tests | cross-tenant suite (NFR-MT-06) | plus a pooled-connection test that alternates tenants on reused connections through RDS Proxy, and an architecture test banning session-level SET | plus pooled-connection test | none added |
| R1, R2 | approved (D2, D3) | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |
| R4–R7 | pending | pending | pending | pending |
Question D4:
D4 — R3: How should every database call carry the tenant setting?
Project/branch/task: main, engineering review of docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md (08-architecture.md §5.2, NFR-MT-02).
ELI10: The database only shows a tenant its own rows when each request first says "I am tenant X". The plan sets that inside a transaction, but doesn't say every read actually runs inside one. A read outside one forgets who it is and errors. If someone "fixes" that by setting it for the whole connection, the next customer on that pooled connection could see the previous tenant's data.
Stakes if we pick wrong: either random errors on reads, or the one bug a multi-tenant product can't survive: one coworking space seeing another's customers.
Recommendation: A because it makes the safe path the only path and proves it with a pooled-connection test.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10, C=5/10
Pros / cons:
A) Transaction per request (recommended)
  ✅ Every query runs with the right tenant; a missing setting returns no rows instead of an error
  ✅ A pooled-connection test and a ban on session-level SET catch regressions in CI
  ❌ Every request holds a transaction, so long requests must stay short and jobs batch per tenant
B) Set on connection open
  ✅ No need to wrap reads in transactions; fewer code paths to remember
  ✅ The pooled-connection test still catches leaks if reset fails
  ❌ RDS Proxy pins every connection, losing pooling; a missed reset leaks tenant data
C) Leave as written
  ✅ No extra design or test work now
  ✅ Still fails closed: a missing setting errors rather than leaking
  ❌ Reads outside a transaction error at random; the fix is left to whoever hits it first
Net: a small rule plus two tests now, against guessing later on the riskiest code in the system.
Header: Tenant setting
Options:
A) Transaction per request (recommended)
Middleware/interceptor opens an explicit transaction for every DB unit of work and runs set_config('app.tenant_id', id, true) first; policies use current_setting('app.tenant_id', true) so a missing setting returns zero rows; add a pooled-connection leak test through RDS Proxy and an architecture test banning session-level SET. Effort S (human: ~1 day / CC: ~1h). Low risk.
B) Set on connection open
Apply the tenant at connection open as a session setting and reset it when the connection returns to the pool; add the pooled-connection leak test. Effort S (human: ~1 day / CC: ~1h). High risk: RDS Proxy pinning and leak on a missed reset.
C) Leave as written
Keep 08-architecture.md §5.2 unchanged; no new tests. Effort S (zero implementation work now). Medium risk: read errors and an unspecified fix.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Transaction per request (recommended), D4 answered 2026-11-03
Accepted scope: every DB unit of work (API request, job step) runs in an explicit transaction opened by middleware/interceptor with set_config('app.tenant_id', id, true) as its first statement; RLS policies use current_setting('app.tenant_id', true) so a missing setting returns zero rows; jobs commit per tenant; add a pooled-connection leak test through RDS Proxy alternating tenants on reused connections, and an architecture test banning session-level SET; update 08-architecture.md §5.2 to match
History: none

### R5: pass/fail criteria for the CloudFront multi-tenant routing spike
Finding: A3, P2, confidence 6/10, docs/requirements/08-architecture.md:180 and :190, reviewer: plan-eng-review
Plan baseline: D15 "SaaS Manager, subject to spike" (13-risks-open-questions-decisions.md:76, Proposed); routing via CloudFront Function + KeyValueStore (08 §6); rollback live within 60 seconds (FR-TA-044)
Runtime evidence: AWS docs confirm multi-tenant distributions with per-tenant certificates, WAF and origin parameters; support for CloudFront Functions and KeyValueStore on them was not confirmed by search (unknown)
Comparison grid:
| Choice | Current | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R5 spike criteria | "validate quotas, pricing per tenant and KeyValueStore integration" | 3-day time-boxed sprint-0 spike must prove: viewer-request Function reading KVS on distribution tenants; managed cert issued within 30 min of CNAME; quotas fit 1,500 hosts (tenants per distribution, KVS size); cost per tenant recorded | no KVS: route by distribution-tenant origin-path parameter (tenants/{id}/v{n}), publish/rollback update the tenant parameter | unchanged, open-ended spike |
| R5 fallback | unspecified | any criterion fails: Caddy on-demand TLS edge (08 §7.1 option C) | Caddy edge if parameters don't fit | unspecified |
| R5 rollback timing (FR-TA-044) | within 60 s | within 60 s (KVS) | depends on tenant-update propagation (unknown, likely minutes) | within 60 s if KVS works |
| D15 status | Proposed | stays Proposed until the spike passes | stays Proposed | stays Proposed |
| R1–R3 | approved | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |
| R4, R6, R7 | pending | pending | pending | pending |
Question D5:
D5 — R5: What must the CloudFront routing spike prove before we commit to it?
Project/branch/task: main, engineering review of docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md (08-architecture.md §6–§7, decision D15).
ELI10: Every tenant website is routed at AWS's edge by a tiny function that looks up "which tenant owns this domain". The plan puts all custom domains on one new AWS feature (multi-tenant CloudFront), but nobody has confirmed that feature runs that lookup function. The spike is meant to check, but has no pass/fail line.
Stakes if we pick wrong: discovering in month 4 that routing or one-minute rollbacks don't work forces an edge redesign while pilots are waiting.
Recommendation: A because a short spike with clear pass/fail and a named fallback settles the riskiest infrastructure bet in week 1.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=7/10, C=4/10
Pros / cons:
A) Spike with exit criteria (recommended)
  ✅ Four concrete checks decide D15 in sprint 0, with Caddy named as the fallback
  ✅ Keeps the 60-second rollback that uses KeyValueStore
  ❌ About 3 days of DevOps time in sprint 0
B) Skip KVS, use tenant params
  ✅ Uses only documented multi-tenant features, so less unknown
  ✅ No KeyValueStore size limits to manage
  ❌ Publish and rollback wait on tenant updates, likely minutes, breaking the 60-second rollback
C) Leave spike open-ended
  ✅ No change to the plan now
  ✅ Spike can still find problems
  ❌ No pass/fail line, so a weak result can slip into production design
Net: three days of sprint-0 time against finding an edge-routing problem after pilots start.
Header: Routing spike
Options:
A) Spike with exit criteria (recommended)
3-day sprint-0 spike must prove: a viewer-request CloudFront Function reading KeyValueStore works on distribution tenants; managed certs issue within 30 min of CNAME; quotas fit 1,500 hosts; cost per tenant recorded. If any fails, fall back to the Caddy on-demand TLS edge. D15 stays Proposed until it passes. Effort S (human: ~3 days / CC: ~1 day). Low risk.
B) Skip KVS, use tenant params
Route by a distribution-tenant origin-path parameter (tenants/{id}/v{n}); publish and rollback update that parameter. Caddy edge if parameters don't fit. Rollback timing becomes unknown, likely minutes. Effort S (human: ~2 days / CC: ~1 day). Medium risk.
C) Leave spike open-ended
Keep the current spike wording with no pass/fail criteria or named fallback. Effort S (zero implementation work now). Medium-high risk.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Spike with exit criteria (recommended), D5 answered 2026-11-03
Accepted scope: 3-day time-boxed sprint-0 spike must prove (1) a viewer-request CloudFront Function reading KeyValueStore works on distribution tenants, (2) managed certificates issue within 30 minutes of the CNAME, (3) quotas fit 1,500 hosts (tenants per distribution, KVS size), (4) cost per tenant is recorded; any failure falls back to the Caddy on-demand TLS edge (08 §7.1 option C); D15 stays Proposed until the spike passes

### R4: concurrent messaging-wallet debits and the −₹500 OTP floor
Finding: C1, P2, confidence 7/10, docs/requirements/09-data-model.md:269, reviewer: plan-eng-review
Plan baseline: Pilot Rules #10 "Sign-in OTPs still go out, with the wallet allowed down to −₹500"; notifications fall back to email at zero balance (approved 2026-11-02)
Runtime evidence: no code exists; 09:269 "MessagingWallet: tenant_id, balance. WalletTransaction: tenant_id, amount (+top-up / −usage) ..." with no debit rule
Comparison grid:
| Choice | Current | A | B |
|---|---|---|---|
| R4 debit mechanism | unspecified | one atomic conditional update per message: UPDATE wallet SET balance = balance - cost WHERE tenant_id = :t AND balance - cost >= :floor RETURNING balance; WalletTransaction written in the same transaction | debit after the provider accepts the send, then reconcile nightly |
| R4 floors | 0 notifications, −₹500 OTP | 0 for notifications, −₹500 for OTP, enforced by the WHERE clause | same floors, checked before send, may be overshot |
| R4 failed debit | unspecified | notification falls back to email; OTP falls back to email OTP | message already sent; overspend recorded |
| R4 tests | none | concurrency test firing parallel sends at a near-floor balance asserts no overspend | reconciliation test |
| R1–R3, R5 | approved | unchanged | unchanged |
| R6, R7 | pending | pending | pending |
Question D6:
D6 — R4: How should messaging-wallet debits stay within their floors?
Project/branch/task: main, engineering review of docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md (Pilot Rules #10, 09-data-model.md §3.10).
ELI10: Each SMS or WhatsApp costs money from the tenant's prepaid wallet. Notifications stop at ₹0, and sign-in codes can go down to −₹500. If two messages go out at the same instant, both can see the same balance and both get sent, so the wallet overshoots its floor. During a burst of sign-ins this can happen many times.
Stakes if we pick wrong: SSPL quietly pays for messages beyond the agreed floor, and tenants see balances that don't add up.
Recommendation: A because one database statement both checks and debits, so it can never overshoot.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=6/10
Pros / cons:
A) Atomic conditional debit (recommended)
  ✅ Check and debit happen in one statement, so parallel sends can never pass the floor
  ✅ The ledger row is written in the same transaction, so balances always reconcile
  ❌ One extra database write before every message send
B) Debit after send
  ✅ The debit uses the provider's actual accepted cost
  ✅ Simpler send path with no database write first
  ❌ Parallel sends overshoot the floor; overspend is only found by a nightly job
Net: one write per message against overspend SSPL absorbs during bursts.
Header: Wallet debits
Options:
A) Atomic conditional debit (recommended)
Each send first runs one conditional UPDATE that debits only if the balance stays at or above its floor (0 for notifications, −₹500 for OTP), writing the WalletTransaction in the same transaction; a failed debit falls back to email or email OTP. Add a parallel-send concurrency test. Effort S (human: ~1 day / CC: ~1h). Low risk.
B) Debit after send
Check the balance, send, then debit the provider's accepted cost; a nightly job reconciles and records overspend. Effort S (human: ~1 day / CC: ~1h). Medium risk: floors can be overshot.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Atomic conditional debit (recommended), D6 answered 2026-11-03
Accepted scope: each message send first runs one conditional UPDATE that debits only if balance - cost stays at or above the floor (0 for notifications, −₹500 for OTP) and writes the WalletTransaction in the same transaction; a failed debit falls back to email (notifications) or email OTP (sign-in); add a parallel-send concurrency test at a near-floor balance asserting no overspend
History: none

Finding: T1, P2, confidence 9/10, CLAUDE.md (no Testing section) and repo root (no test config), reviewer: plan-eng-review

Comparison grid:
| Choice | Current | A | B |
|---|---|---|---|
| R6 .NET unit/integration | unspecified | xUnit + FluentAssertions; Testcontainers PostgreSQL 17 (real RLS, btree_gist, exclusion constraints) via WebApplicationFactory | xUnit with EF Core InMemory / SQLite provider |
| R6 React | unspecified | Vitest + React Testing Library | Vitest + React Testing Library |
| R6 E2E | Playwright + axe (08 §15) | Playwright + axe against staging with Razorpay test mode | Playwright + axe |
| R6 architecture | NetArchTest (NFR-OT-03) | NetArchTest | NetArchTest |
| R1–R5 | approved | unchanged | unchanged |
| R7 | pending | pending | pending |
Question D7:
D7 — R6: Which test stack should the .NET backend use for database-backed tests?
Project/branch/task: main, engineering review of docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md (08-architecture.md §15, NFR-OT-03).
ELI10: The riskiest rules in Cowos live in PostgreSQL itself: tenant isolation (row-level security), no double bookings (an exclusion constraint), gap-free invoice numbers. Tests can run against a real PostgreSQL in a throwaway container, or against a fake in-memory database. The fake is faster but doesn't have those PostgreSQL features at all.
Stakes if we pick wrong: tests pass while tenant isolation or double-booking protection is broken in the real database.
Recommendation: A because only a real PostgreSQL can prove RLS, the exclusion constraint and the pooled-connection test from D4.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=4/10
Pros / cons:
A) Real Postgres in tests (recommended)
  ✅ Exercises RLS, btree_gist exclusion constraints and transactions exactly as production
  ✅ Supports the pooled-connection leak test and wallet concurrency test already approved
  ❌ Needs Docker in CI and locally; integration tests run slower (seconds, not milliseconds)
B) In-memory database
  ✅ Very fast tests with no Docker dependency
  ✅ Simple to set up for pure domain logic
  ❌ No RLS, exclusion constraints or real transactions, so the riskiest rules go untested
Net: Docker in CI against tests that can't see the rules most likely to hurt tenants.
Header: Test stack
Options:
A) Real Postgres in tests (recommended)
xUnit + FluentAssertions; integration tests use Testcontainers PostgreSQL 17 through WebApplicationFactory so RLS, exclusion constraints and transactions are real; Vitest + React Testing Library for React; Playwright + axe for E2E on staging with Razorpay test mode; NetArchTest for module boundaries. Effort S (human: ~1 day setup / CC: ~1h). Low risk.
B) In-memory database
xUnit with EF Core InMemory or SQLite for backend tests; same Vitest, Playwright + axe and NetArchTest. RLS, exclusion constraints and the D4/D6 tests can't run. Effort S (human: ~half day / CC: ~30min). High risk.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Real Postgres in tests (recommended), D7 answered 2026-11-03
Accepted scope: xUnit + FluentAssertions; integration tests on Testcontainers PostgreSQL 17 via WebApplicationFactory (real RLS, btree_gist exclusion constraints, transactions); Vitest + React Testing Library for the React apps; Playwright + axe E2E on staging with Razorpay test mode; NetArchTest for module boundaries; Docker available in CI and locally; add a `## Testing` section to CLAUDE.md with the commands once the projects exist

### R7: indexes for the cross-tenant booking sweeps
Finding: P1, P3, confidence 7/10, docs/requirements/08-architecture.md:280/283/284, reviewer: plan-eng-review
Plan baseline: sweeps every 30 s (hold expiry), 5 min (reminders), 15 min (no-show); booking indexes lead with tenant_id (09 §3.4, NFR-MT-01)
Runtime evidence: no code; scale estimate ~450k bookings/month at design load [estimated: 300/tenant × 1,500 tenants, NFR-PERF-11]; tenant-leading indexes can't serve a cross-tenant predicate
Comparison grid:
| Choice | Current | A | B |
|---|---|---|---|
| R7 sweep indexes | none specified | partial indexes: booking(hold_expires_at) WHERE status = 'Held'; booking(starts_at) WHERE status IN ('Confirmed','AwaitingPayment'); booking(ends_at) WHERE status = 'Confirmed' for no-show | none; rely on tenant-leading indexes |
| R7 test | none | query-plan check in an integration test asserts each sweep uses its partial index | none |
| R1–R6 | approved | unchanged | unchanged |
| R8 sweep access under RLS | pending | pending | pending |
Question D8:
D8 — R7: Should the booking sweeps get their own partial indexes?
Project/branch/task: main, engineering review of docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md (08-architecture.md §12, 09-data-model.md §3.4).
ELI10: Background jobs look for expiring holds every 30 seconds, upcoming reminders every 5 minutes and no-shows every 15 minutes. All booking indexes start with the tenant, so these all-tenant searches can't use them and read the whole bookings table every time. Small "only the rows that matter" indexes make each check instant.
Stakes if we pick wrong: as bookings grow, the 30-second sweep gets slower and competes with customers checking availability.
Recommendation: A because three tiny indexes keep the sweeps fast forever at almost no write cost.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=5/10
Pros / cons:
A) Add partial indexes (recommended)
  ✅ Each sweep reads only the handful of matching rows, whatever the table size
  ✅ A query-plan test catches an index being dropped or bypassed
  ❌ Three more indexes to maintain; tiny extra cost on booking writes
B) No extra indexes
  ✅ Nothing extra to add or maintain now
  ✅ Fine while the table is small during the pilot
  ❌ Full-table reads every 30 seconds once bookings reach the hundreds of thousands
Net: three small indexes now against a sweep that slows down as the product succeeds.
Header: Sweep indexes
Options:
A) Add partial indexes (recommended)
Partial indexes on booking(hold_expires_at) WHERE status = 'Held', booking(starts_at) WHERE status IN ('Confirmed','AwaitingPayment'), and booking(ends_at) WHERE status = 'Confirmed'; an integration test asserts each sweep's query plan uses its index. Effort S (human: ~2h / CC: ~15min). Low risk.
B) No extra indexes
Rely on the existing tenant-leading indexes; revisit if sweeps slow down. Effort S (zero implementation work now). Medium risk at scale.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Add partial indexes (recommended), D8 answered 2026-11-03
Accepted scope: partial indexes booking(hold_expires_at) WHERE status = 'Held', booking(starts_at) WHERE status IN ('Confirmed','AwaitingPayment'), booking(ends_at) WHERE status = 'Confirmed'; an integration test asserts each sweep's query plan uses its index

### R8: how cross-tenant sweeps find work under row-level security
Finding: P2, P2, confidence 8/10, docs/requirements/08-architecture.md:280 with R3 (D4), reviewer: plan-eng-review
Plan baseline: D4 every transaction runs as one tenant with RLS, jobs commit per tenant; app role NOBYPASSRLS (NFR-MT-02); sweeps every 30 s / 5 min / 15 min (08 §12); R7 partial indexes (D8)
Runtime evidence: no code; under D4 a sweep without a tenant setting sees zero rows, so the plan has no stated way to find expired holds, due reminders or no-shows across tenants
Comparison grid:
| Choice | Current | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| R8 finding work | unspecified | narrow `cowos_sweeper` role whose read-only RLS policy exposes only (id, tenant_id) of rows matching each sweep predicate; worker then processes each row in a per-tenant transaction (D4) | schedule a delayed Hangfire job per hold/booking at creation with tenant_id in the payload (cancel/reschedule on change); hourly per-tenant safety sweep | every sweep iterates all tenants, setting each tenant and querying |
| R8 DB roles | cowos_app, cowos_platform | adds cowos_sweeper (SELECT on id, tenant_id via policy; no writes) | none added | none added |
| R8 load at 1,500 tenants | n/a | 1 indexed query per sweep | one job per hold/booking event plus 1,500 queries/hour | ~50 queries/s for the 30-second sweep [estimated: 1,500 / 30 s] |
| R8 tests | none | isolation test proves the sweeper role can't read other columns or write; sweep finds rows across two tenants | job cancel/reschedule tests; safety sweep test | sweep test across two tenants |
| R1–R7 | approved | unchanged | unchanged | unchanged |
Question D9:
D9 — R8: How should background sweeps find work across tenants?
Project/branch/task: main, engineering review of docs/designs/cowos-platform-v1.md (08-architecture.md §12, D4, D8).
ELI10: After D4, every database session acts as exactly one tenant, which keeps data safe. But jobs like "release expired holds" need to look across all 1,500 tenants, and a one-tenant session sees nothing. They need a narrow way to find the work, then handle each item as its own tenant.
Stakes if we pick wrong: holds never expire and reminders never send, or a broad "see everything" role weakens the isolation D4 just locked in.
Recommendation: A because one read-only role that sees only IDs keeps isolation tight while sweeps stay a single fast query.
Completeness: A=10/10, B=8/10, C=7/10
Pros / cons:
A) Narrow sweeper role (recommended)
  ✅ One indexed query per sweep; each item is then handled safely as its own tenant
  ✅ The role can only read IDs of matching rows, and an isolation test proves it
  ❌ One more database role and policy to review and keep tight
B) Per-hold scheduled jobs
  ✅ No cross-tenant query at all; each job already knows its tenant
  ✅ Work runs exactly on time instead of up to 30 s late
  ❌ Thousands of delayed jobs to cancel and reschedule on every change, plus a safety sweep anyway
C) Loop over every tenant
  ✅ No new role or job type; reuses the D4 path exactly
  ✅ Simplest to reason about for isolation
  ❌ About 50 queries a second at 1,500 tenants just to find a few expired holds
Net: one tightly scoped role against either job sprawl or a constant query load.
Header: Sweep access
Options:
A) Narrow sweeper role (recommended)
Add a cowos_sweeper DB role whose read-only RLS policy exposes only (id, tenant_id) of rows matching each sweep predicate (uses the D8 partial indexes); the worker processes each row in its own per-tenant transaction (D4). Isolation test proves the role can't read other columns or write. Effort S (human: ~1 day / CC: ~1h). Low risk.
B) Per-hold scheduled jobs
Schedule a delayed Hangfire job per hold and booking at creation, with tenant_id in the payload; cancel or reschedule it on every change; add an hourly per-tenant safety sweep. Effort M (human: ~3 days / CC: ~3h). Medium risk.
C) Loop over every tenant
Each sweep run iterates all tenants, sets the tenant and queries; no new role or job type. Effort S (human: ~half day / CC: ~30min). Medium risk: ~50 queries/s at 1,500 tenants.

State: approved
Actual answer: A) Narrow sweeper role (recommended), D9 answered 2026-11-03
Accepted scope: add DB role cowos_sweeper whose read-only RLS policies expose only (id, tenant_id) of rows matching each sweep predicate (hold expiry, reminders, no-show; uses the D8 partial indexes); the worker processes each returned row in its own per-tenant transaction (D4); isolation test proves the role can't read other columns or write, and a sweep test finds rows across two tenants
History: none

### R9: TODO, revisit the equal split of the AWS bill
